It’s just constant worrying, and it’s starting to feel like I can’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about the situation at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely not just work. Work kind of starts it, like the lab partnership thing and whether my data are off, but then my brain just… keeps going. Bills, my family thinking I’m not doing enough, grad school applications—like I’ve somehow already messed that up before I’ve even really started.\n\nIt jumps around a lot. I’ll mean to focus on one thing and then I’m suddenly worrying about five other things that are only kind of connected.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after the new partnership at your lab was announced?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I mean, it wasn’t like the exact day they announced it I suddenly changed, but it’s been going on about seven months, since the funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run.\n\nI remember because around then everyone was talking in the break room about what it meant, and I kept trying to act like I wasn’t listening, but I was. And then it just sort of… spread from there.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very hard. I’ll try to kind of talk myself back into one thing, like, “just finish the report,” or “just check the assay,” but then one worry catches on another one.\n\nAnd then I’m not even really doing the task anymore. I’m just staring at the screen, and my head is running through every possible way it could go wrong. Like I’ll read the same line three times and somehow end up thinking about something completely different, and then I have to remind myself what I was even supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When these worries hit, do you find yourself repeatedly checking things to feel sure nothing is wrong?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do look back at things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it repeatedly checking to feel sure. Like, I might reread something and then get distracted thinking about whether I worded it weirdly, and then somehow I’m thinking about this email I sent a week ago, or whether my roommate paid me back for groceries—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess… I haven’t really noticed a specific checking thing. It’s more like I get stuck in the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Before all of this recent worrying started, did you have trouble paying attention or staying organized going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? I’d procrastinate on stuff sometimes, but everyone did. I don’t know if it was a “since childhood” kind of thing.\n\nMy mom used to keep this big calendar on the fridge with everyone’s stuff on it, and I guess I depended on that more than I thought, but—sorry, I’m not sure that answers it. I don’t really remember having some obvious problem with attention or organization before all this.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. Especially at work I feel kind of… wound up, like I can’t just sit in my chair normally. I’ll be tapping my foot or picking up my phone to check messages even when there’s no reason, and then I’ll get up and walk back to the instrument room like I needed something, but I don’t even really need to be there.\n\nAnd normal lab noises will make me jump sometimes, which is embarrassing because it’s just the lab doing lab things, you know? I’ll mean to focus on one thing and then I’m suddenly worrying about five other things that are only kind of connected.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after the new partnership at your lab was announced?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I mean, it wasn’t like the exact day they announced it I suddenly changed, but it’s been going on about seven months, since the funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run.\n\nI remember because around then everyone was talking in the break room about what it meant, and I kept trying to act like I wasn’t listening, but I was. And then it just sort of… spread from there.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very hard. I’ll try to kind of talk myself back into one thing, like, “just finish the report,” or “just check the assay,” but then one worry catches on another one.\n\nAnd then I’m not even really doing the task anymore. I’m just staring at the screen, and my head is running through every possible way it could go wrong. Like I’ll read the same line three times and somehow end up thinking about something completely different, and then I have to remind myself what I was even supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When these worries hit, do you find yourself repeatedly checking things to feel sure nothing is wrong?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do look back at things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it repeatedly checking to feel sure. Like, I might reread something and then get distracted thinking about whether I worded it weirdly, and then somehow I’m thinking about this email I sent a week ago, or whether my roommate paid me back for groceries—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess… I haven’t really noticed a specific checking thing. It’s more like I get stuck in the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Before all of this recent worrying started, did you have trouble paying attention or staying organized going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? I’d procrastinate on stuff sometimes, but everyone did. I don’t know if it was a “since childhood” kind of thing.\n\nMy mom used to keep this big calendar on the fridge with everyone’s stuff on it, and I guess I depended on that more than I thought, but—sorry, I’m not sure that answers it. I don’t really remember having some obvious problem with attention or organization before all this.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. Especially at work I feel kind of… wound up, like I can’t just sit in my chair normally. I’ll be tapping my foot or picking up my phone to check messages even when there’s no reason, and then I’ll get up and walk back to the instrument room like I needed something, but I don’t even really need to be there.\n\nAnd normal lab noises will make me jump sometimes, which is embarrassing because it’s just the lab doing lab things, you know? Like a door closing or something clicking and I’m suddenly on alert.\n\nDoctor: If the situation at work were resolved, do you think most of this worrying would go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, but I don’t think so. Work is a big part of it, like with the new lab partnership and worrying they’re going to cut my position, or that my data are wrong and I’ve messed something up.\n\nBut then my mind just jumps to other stuff—like bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind, or whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school. Even when one thing gets answered, there’s another thing right behind it. So… no, I don’t think fixing work would make most of it go away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah. I came in because I can’t seem to get my mind to stay on one thing without it turning into ten other things. Like at work, I’m worrying our lab’s new partnership might mean my position gets cut, and then I’m thinking maybe my data are wrong, and then it jumps to bills piling up, and whether my family thinks I’m falling behind.\n\nAnd then—this sounds dramatic—but I start thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school somehow. I was looking at programs the other night and ended up reorganizing old notes from a chemistry class instead of actually applying, which… isn’t really the point. It’s just constant worrying, and it’s starting to feel like I can’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about the situation at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely not just work. Work kind of starts it, like the lab partnership thing and whether my data are off, but then my brain just… keeps going. Bills, my family thinking I’m not doing enough, grad school applications—like I’ve somehow already messed that up before I’ve even really started.\n\nIt jumps around a lot. I’ll mean to focus on one thing and then I’m suddenly worrying about five other things that are only kind of connected.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after the new partnership at your lab was announced?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I mean, it wasn’t like the exact day they announced it I suddenly changed, but it’s been going on about seven months, since the funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run.\n\nI remember because around then everyone was talking in the break room about what it meant, and I kept trying to act like I wasn’t listening, but I was. And then it just sort of… spread from there.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very hard. I’ll try to kind of talk myself back into one thing, like, “just finish the report,” or “just check the assay,” but then one worry catches on another one.\n\nAnd then I’m not even really doing the task anymore. I mean, I do look back at things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it repeatedly checking to feel sure. Like, I might reread something and then get distracted thinking about whether I worded it weirdly, and then somehow I’m thinking about this email I sent a week ago, or whether my roommate paid me back for groceries—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess… I haven’t really noticed a specific checking thing. It’s more like I get stuck in the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Before all of this recent worrying started, did you have trouble paying attention or staying organized going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t know if it was a “since childhood” kind of thing.\n\nMy mom used to keep this big calendar on the fridge with everyone’s stuff on it, and I guess I depended on that more than I thought, but—sorry, I’m not sure that answers it. I’ll be sitting there with spreadsheets open, and then realize I’ve been clenching my jaw the whole time, which is weird because it’s not like the spreadsheet is physically doing anything to me.\n\nBy the evening I’ll have this headache and stiffness, like I spent the day hauling equipment around the lab or moving boxes, except I didn’t. I just… sat there tense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it, I guess. Some nights I’m fine, or at least I think I am, and other nights I look at the clock and start doing the math of how much sleep I’d get if I fell asleep right then, which is probably not the best thing to do.\n\nBut, sorry, that reminds me—my roommate has this lamp that clicks weirdly when it cools down, and for a while I thought it was something in the wall, like an electrical thing, so I went down this whole rabbit hole about whether outlets can make noises. So… sleep, yeah, I’m not totally sure how to answer. It hasn’t been something I’ve measured, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around thinking, like, “I’m depressed,” exactly. I guess… I don’t know if it’s “down or depressed a lot.” I’ve noticed feeling kind of heavy sometimes, but I haven’t really sorted out whether that’s sadness or just being worn out.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling sad most days?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 24 / chemist_or_materials_scientist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. It’s more like I get stuck in the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Before all of this recent worrying started, did you have trouble paying attention or staying organized going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t know if it was a “since childhood” kind of thing.\n\nMy mom used to keep this big calendar on the fridge with everyone’s stuff on it, and I guess I depended on that more than I thought, but—sorry, I’m not sure that answers it. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, tired, definitely. Like, I can be in bed a full night, and I still wake up feeling like I already pulled an all-nighter, which is annoying because then I’m trying to start the day already behind.

At work, even something simple like labeling samples can feel like it takes way more effort than it should. And then I get home and try to cook dinner for my family, and it’s like my body is heavy and my brain is just… drained. I’ll stand there with a pan out and forget what step I was on. Well, not forget exactly, just feel too wiped to keep moving smoothly.

Work kind of starts it, like the lab partnership thing and whether my data are off, but then my brain just… keeps going. Bills, my family thinking I’m not doing enough, grad school applications—like I’ve somehow already messed that up before I’ve even really started.\n\nIt jumps around a lot. I’ll mean to focus on one thing and then I’m suddenly worrying about five other things that are only kind of connected.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after the new partnership at your lab was announced?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I mean, it wasn’t like the exact day they announced it I suddenly changed, but it’s been going on about seven months, since the funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run.\n\nI remember because around then everyone was talking in the break room about what it meant, and I kept trying to act like I wasn’t listening, but I was. And then it just sort of… spread from there.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very hard. I’ll try to kind of talk myself back into one thing, like, “just finish the report,” or “just check the assay,” but then one worry catches on another one.\n\nAnd then I’m not even really doing the task anymore. I’m just staring at the screen, and my head is running through every possible way it could go wrong. Like I’ll read the same line three times and somehow end up thinking about something completely different, and then I have to remind myself what I was even supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When these worries hit, do you find yourself repeatedly checking things to feel sure nothing is wrong?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do look back at things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it repeatedly checking to feel sure. Like, I might reread something and then get distracted thinking about whether I worded it weirdly, and then somehow I’m thinking about this email I sent a week ago, or whether my roommate paid me back for groceries—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess… I haven’t really noticed a specific checking thing. It’s more like I get stuck in the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Before all of this recent worrying started, did you have trouble paying attention or staying organized going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? I’d procrastinate on stuff sometimes, but everyone did. I don’t know if it was a “since childhood” kind of thing.\n\nMy mom used to keep this big calendar on the fridge with everyone’s stuff on it, and I guess I depended on that more than I thought, but—sorry, I’m not sure that answers it. I don’t really remember having some obvious problem with attention or organization before all this.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. Especially at work I feel kind of… wound up, like I can’t just sit in my chair normally. I’ll be tapping my foot or picking up my phone to check messages even when there’s no reason, and then I’ll get up and walk back to the instrument room like I needed something, but I don’t even really need to be there.\n\nAnd normal lab noises will make me jump sometimes, which is embarrassing because it’s just the lab doing lab things, you know? Like a door closing or something clicking and I’m suddenly on alert.\n\nDoctor: If the situation at work were resolved, do you think most of this worrying would go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, but I don’t think so. I just… sat there tense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it, I guess. Some nights I’m fine, or at least I think I am, and other nights I look at the clock and start doing the math of how much sleep I’d get if I fell asleep right then, which is probably not the best thing to do.\n\nBut, sorry, that reminds me—my roommate has this lamp that clicks weirdly when it cools down, and for a while I thought it was something in the wall, like an electrical thing, so I went down this whole rabbit hole about whether outlets can make noises. I don’t really keep track of it that way.\n\nLike, I can tell you if a sample changed color or if an instrument reading drifted, because there’s a number or something to point to, but with sadness it’s more slippery. Like, I can be in bed a full night, and I still wake up feeling like I already pulled an all-nighter, which is annoying because then I’m trying to start the day already behind.\n\nAt work, even something simple like labeling samples can feel like it takes way more effort than it should. And then I get home and try to cook dinner for my family, and it’s like my body is heavy and my brain is just… drained. I’ll stand there with a pan out and forget what step I was on. Well, not forget exactly, just feel too wiped to keep moving smoothly.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 24 / chemist_or_materials_scientist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I’d say nearly every day. “Down” is kind of broad, you know? Like sometimes I’ll just be sitting there and everything feels… gray, I guess, but then I’ll get distracted by some work thing, like a batch number not matching or a weird email, and I’m more focused on that than on whether I’m depressed.

Hopeless feels like a big word. I don’t know. I haven’t really been counting days. The past two weeks have just sort of blurred together.

Like at work, I’m worrying our lab’s new partnership might mean my position gets cut, and then I’m thinking maybe my data are wrong, and then it jumps to bills piling up, and whether my family thinks I’m falling behind.\n\nAnd then—this sounds dramatic—but I start thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school somehow. I was looking at programs the other night and ended up reorganizing old notes from a chemistry class instead of actually applying, which… isn’t really the point. It’s just constant worrying, and it’s starting to feel like I can’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about the situation at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely not just work. Work kind of starts it, like the lab partnership thing and whether my data are off, but then my brain just… keeps going. Bills, my family thinking I’m not doing enough, grad school applications—like I’ve somehow already messed that up before I’ve even really started.\n\nIt jumps around a lot. I’ll mean to focus on one thing and then I’m suddenly worrying about five other things that are only kind of connected.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after the new partnership at your lab was announced?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I mean, it wasn’t like the exact day they announced it I suddenly changed, but it’s been going on about seven months, since the funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run.\n\nI remember because around then everyone was talking in the break room about what it meant, and I kept trying to act like I wasn’t listening, but I was. And then it just sort of… spread from there.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very hard. I’ll try to kind of talk myself back into one thing, like, “just finish the report,” or “just check the assay,” but then one worry catches on another one.\n\nAnd then I’m not even really doing the task anymore. I’m just staring at the screen, and my head is running through every possible way it could go wrong. Like I’ll read the same line three times and somehow end up thinking about something completely different, and then I have to remind myself what I was even supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When these worries hit, do you find yourself repeatedly checking things to feel sure nothing is wrong?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do look back at things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it repeatedly checking to feel sure. Like, I might reread something and then get distracted thinking about whether I worded it weirdly, and then somehow I’m thinking about this email I sent a week ago, or whether my roommate paid me back for groceries—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess… I haven’t really noticed a specific checking thing. It’s more like I get stuck in the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Before all of this recent worrying started, did you have trouble paying attention or staying organized going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? I’d procrastinate on stuff sometimes, but everyone did. I don’t know if it was a “since childhood” kind of thing.\n\nMy mom used to keep this big calendar on the fridge with everyone’s stuff on it, and I guess I depended on that more than I thought, but—sorry, I’m not sure that answers it. I don’t really remember having some obvious problem with attention or organization before all this.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. Especially at work I feel kind of… wound up, like I can’t just sit in my chair normally. I’ll be tapping my foot or picking up my phone to check messages even when there’s no reason, and then I’ll get up and walk back to the instrument room like I needed something, but I don’t even really need to be there.\n\nAnd normal lab noises will make me jump sometimes, which is embarrassing because it’s just the lab doing lab things, you know? Like a door closing or something clicking and I’m suddenly on alert.\n\nDoctor: If the situation at work were resolved, do you think most of this worrying would go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, but I don’t think so. Work is a big part of it, like with the new lab partnership and worrying they’re going to cut my position, or that my data are wrong and I’ve messed something up.\n\nBut then my mind just jumps to other stuff—like bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind, or whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school. Even when one thing gets answered, there’s another thing right behind it. So… no, I don’t think fixing work would make most of it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly in my shoulders and neck—like they’re just sort of locked up all day. I’ll be sitting there with spreadsheets open, and then realize I’ve been clenching my jaw the whole time, which is weird because it’s not like the spreadsheet is physically doing anything to me.\n\nBy the evening I’ll have this headache and stiffness, like I spent the day hauling equipment around the lab or moving boxes, except I didn’t. I just… sat there tense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it, I guess. Some nights I’m fine, or at least I think I am, and other nights I look at the clock and start doing the math of how much sleep I’d get if I fell asleep right then, which is probably not the best thing to do.\n\nBut, sorry, that reminds me—my roommate has this lamp that clicks weirdly when it cools down, and for a while I thought it was something in the wall, like an electrical thing, so I went down this whole rabbit hole about whether outlets can make noises. So… sleep, yeah, I’m not totally sure how to answer. It hasn’t been something I’ve measured, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around thinking, like, “I’m depressed,” exactly. I guess… I don’t know if it’s “down or depressed a lot.” I’ve noticed feeling kind of heavy sometimes, but I haven’t really sorted out whether that’s sadness or just being worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I… I’m not totally sure. I don’t really keep track of it that way.\n\nLike, I can tell you if a sample changed color or if an instrument reading drifted, because there’s a number or something to point to, but with sadness it’s more slippery. Some days I feel off, but I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. So… I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. My shoulders and neck are kind of… always up here, if that makes sense. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I still do things, but sometimes it feels more like I’m just moving through the list than actually enjoying them.

Like, I’ll put on a show or start messing with something at home, and then I’m thinking about whether I left something unfinished at work, or if I should’ve checked an email again. And then I realize I didn’t really watch the episode. But I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest, or if I’m just distracted. 

“Pleasure” is a weird word, honestly. I guess I haven’t been paying close attention to that.

Like, I can be in bed a full night, and I still wake up feeling like I already pulled an all-nighter, which is annoying because then I’m trying to start the day already behind.\n\nAt work, even something simple like labeling samples can feel like it takes way more effort than it should. And then I get home and try to cook dinner for my family, and it’s like my body is heavy and my brain is just… drained. I’ll stand there with a pan out and forget what step I was on. Well, not forget exactly, just feel too wiped to keep moving smoothly.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say nearly every day. “Down” is kind of broad, you know? Like sometimes I’ll just be sitting there and everything feels… gray, I guess, but then I’ll get distracted by some work thing, like a batch number not matching or a weird email, and I’m more focused on that than on whether I’m depressed.\n\nHopeless feels like a big word. I don’t know. I haven’t really been counting days. The past two weeks have just sort of blurred together.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you often had little interest or pleasure in doing things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I still do things, but sometimes it feels more like I’m just moving through the list than actually enjoying them.\n\nLike, I’ll put on a show or start messing with something at home, and then I’m thinking about whether I left something unfinished at work, or if I should’ve checked an email again. And then I realize I didn’t really watch the episode. But I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest, or if I’m just distracted. \n\n“Pleasure” is a weird word, honestly. I guess I haven’t been paying close attention to that.\n\n[Last Question]\nDuring the past two weeks, have you felt sad or low for most of the day nearly every day?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not sure I can say “most of the day nearly every day.” That sounds really specific, and I don’t think I’ve been tracking it like that.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi… yeah. I came in because I can’t seem to get my mind to stay on one thing without it turning into ten other things. Like at work, I’m worrying our lab’s new partnership might mean my position gets cut, and then I’m thinking maybe my data are wrong, and then it jumps to bills piling up, and whether my family thinks I’m falling behind.\n\nAnd then—this sounds dramatic—but I start thinking I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school somehow. I was looking at programs the other night and ended up reorganizing old notes from a chemistry class instead of actually applying, which… isn’t really the point. It’s just constant worrying, and it’s starting to feel like I can’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about the situation at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely not just work. Work kind of starts it, like the lab partnership thing and whether my data are off, but then my brain just… keeps going. Bills, my family thinking I’m not doing enough, grad school applications—like I’ve somehow already messed that up before I’ve even really started.\n\nIt jumps around a lot. I’ll mean to focus on one thing and then I’m suddenly worrying about five other things that are only kind of connected.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after the new partnership at your lab was announced?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I mean, it wasn’t like the exact day they announced it I suddenly changed, but it’s been going on about seven months, since the funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run.\n\nI remember because around then everyone was talking in the break room about what it meant, and I kept trying to act like I wasn’t listening, but I was. And then it just sort of… spread from there.\n\nDoctor: When the worrying starts, is it hard to stop or control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… very hard. I’ll try to kind of talk myself back into one thing, like, “just finish the report,” or “just check the assay,” but then one worry catches on another one.\n\nAnd then I’m not even really doing the task anymore. I’m just staring at the screen, and my head is running through every possible way it could go wrong. Like I’ll read the same line three times and somehow end up thinking about something completely different, and then I have to remind myself what I was even supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When these worries hit, do you find yourself repeatedly checking things to feel sure nothing is wrong?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do look back at things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it repeatedly checking to feel sure. Like, I might reread something and then get distracted thinking about whether I worded it weirdly, and then somehow I’m thinking about this email I sent a week ago, or whether my roommate paid me back for groceries—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess… I haven’t really noticed a specific checking thing. It’s more like I get stuck in the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Before all of this recent worrying started, did you have trouble paying attention or staying organized going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? I’d procrastinate on stuff sometimes, but everyone did. I don’t know if it was a “since childhood” kind of thing.\n\nMy mom used to keep this big calendar on the fridge with everyone’s stuff on it, and I guess I depended on that more than I thought, but—sorry, I’m not sure that answers it. I don’t really remember having some obvious problem with attention or organization before all this.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. Especially at work I feel kind of… wound up, like I can’t just sit in my chair normally. I’ll be tapping my foot or picking up my phone to check messages even when there’s no reason, and then I’ll get up and walk back to the instrument room like I needed something, but I don’t even really need to be there.\n\nAnd normal lab noises will make me jump sometimes, which is embarrassing because it’s just the lab doing lab things, you know? Like a door closing or something clicking and I’m suddenly on alert.\n\nDoctor: If the situation at work were resolved, do you think most of this worrying would go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, but I don’t think so. Work is a big part of it, like with the new lab partnership and worrying they’re going to cut my position, or that my data are wrong and I’ve messed something up.\n\nBut then my mind just jumps to other stuff—like bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind, or whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school. Even when one thing gets answered, there’s another thing right behind it. So… no, I don’t think fixing work would make most of it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly in my shoulders and neck—like they’re just sort of locked up all day. I’ll be sitting there with spreadsheets open, and then realize I’ve been clenching my jaw the whole time, which is weird because it’s not like the spreadsheet is physically doing anything to me.\n\nBy the evening I’ll have this headache and stiffness, like I spent the day hauling equipment around the lab or moving boxes, except I didn’t. I just… sat there tense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it, I guess. Some nights I’m fine, or at least I think I am, and other nights I look at the clock and start doing the math of how much sleep I’d get if I fell asleep right then, which is probably not the best thing to do.\n\nBut, sorry, that reminds me—my roommate has this lamp that clicks weirdly when it cools down, and for a while I thought it was something in the wall, like an electrical thing, so I went down this whole rabbit hole about whether outlets can make noises. So… sleep, yeah, I’m not totally sure how to answer. It hasn’t been something I’ve measured, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around thinking, like, “I’m depressed,” exactly. Some days I feel pretty low, but then I start wondering if that’s just because everything feels backed up, like when laundry sits too long and then somehow it becomes this whole project.\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess… I don’t know if it’s “down or depressed a lot.” I’ve noticed feeling kind of heavy sometimes, but I haven’t really sorted out whether that’s sadness or just being worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I… I’m not totally sure. I don’t really keep track of it that way.\n\nLike, I can tell you if a sample changed color or if an instrument reading drifted, because there’s a number or something to point to, but with sadness it’s more slippery. Some days I feel off, but I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. So… I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. My shoulders and neck are kind of… always up here, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be looking at spreadsheets and suddenly realize my jaw is clenched so hard it feels ridiculous.\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll think I’ve been sitting still all day, but by the evening I have this stiffness and headache like I was carrying equipment around the lab or hauling boxes or something. I was looking at programs the other night and ended up reorganizing old notes from a chemistry class instead of actually applying, which… isn’t really the point. It’s just constant worrying, and it’s starting to feel like I can’t shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about many different parts of your life, rather than mainly about the situation at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s definitely not just work. Work kind of starts it, like the lab partnership thing and whether my data are off, but then my brain just… keeps going. Bills, my family thinking I’m not doing enough, grad school applications—like I’ve somehow already messed that up before I’ve even really started.\n\nIt jumps around a lot. I’ll mean to focus on one thing and then I’m suddenly worrying about five other things that are only kind of connected.\n\nDoctor: Did these worries start after the new partnership at your lab was announced?\n\nPatient: Kind of, yeah. I mean, it wasn’t like the exact day they announced it I suddenly changed, but it’s been going on about seven months, since the funding shifted and that commercial partnership changed how everything was being run.\n\nI remember because around then everyone was talking in the break room about what it meant, and I kept trying to act like I wasn’t listening, but I was. Like I’ll read the same line three times and somehow end up thinking about something completely different, and then I have to remind myself what I was even supposed to be doing.\n\nDoctor: When these worries hit, do you find yourself repeatedly checking things to feel sure nothing is wrong?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I do look back at things sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it repeatedly checking to feel sure. Like, I might reread something and then get distracted thinking about whether I worded it weirdly, and then somehow I’m thinking about this email I sent a week ago, or whether my roommate paid me back for groceries—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nI guess… I haven’t really noticed a specific checking thing. It’s more like I get stuck in the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Before all of this recent worrying started, did you have trouble paying attention or staying organized going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? I’d procrastinate on stuff sometimes, but everyone did. I don’t know if it was a “since childhood” kind of thing.\n\nMy mom used to keep this big calendar on the fridge with everyone’s stuff on it, and I guess I depended on that more than I thought, but—sorry, I’m not sure that answers it. I don’t really remember having some obvious problem with attention or organization before all this.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. Especially at work I feel kind of… wound up, like I can’t just sit in my chair normally. Like a door closing or something clicking and I’m suddenly on alert.\n\nDoctor: If the situation at work were resolved, do you think most of this worrying would go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, but I don’t think so. Work is a big part of it, like with the new lab partnership and worrying they’re going to cut my position, or that my data are wrong and I’ve messed something up.\n\nBut then my mind just jumps to other stuff—like bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind, or whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school. Even when one thing gets answered, there’s another thing right behind it. So… no, I don’t think fixing work would make most of it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly in my shoulders and neck—like they’re just sort of locked up all day. I’ll be sitting there with spreadsheets open, and then realize I’ve been clenching my jaw the whole time, which is weird because it’s not like the spreadsheet is physically doing anything to me.\n\nBy the evening I’ll have this headache and stiffness, like I spent the day hauling equipment around the lab or moving boxes, except I didn’t. I just… sat there tense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it, I guess. Some nights I’m fine, or at least I think I am, and other nights I look at the clock and start doing the math of how much sleep I’d get if I fell asleep right then, which is probably not the best thing to do.\n\nBut, sorry, that reminds me—my roommate has this lamp that clicks weirdly when it cools down, and for a while I thought it was something in the wall, like an electrical thing, so I went down this whole rabbit hole about whether outlets can make noises. So… sleep, yeah, I’m not totally sure how to answer. It hasn’t been something I’ve measured, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I… I’m not totally sure. I don’t really keep track of it that way.\n\nLike, I can tell you if a sample changed color or if an instrument reading drifted, because there’s a number or something to point to, but with sadness it’s more slippery. My shoulders and neck are kind of… always up here, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be looking at spreadsheets and suddenly realize my jaw is clenched so hard it feels ridiculous.\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll think I’ve been sitting still all day, but by the evening I have this stiffness and headache like I was carrying equipment around the lab or hauling boxes or something. Work is a big part of it, like with the new lab partnership and worrying they’re going to cut my position, or that my data are wrong and I’ve messed something up.\n\nBut then my mind just jumps to other stuff—like bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind, or whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school. Even when one thing gets answered, there’s another thing right behind it. So… no, I don’t think fixing work would make most of it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly in my shoulders and neck—like they’re just sort of locked up all day. I’ll be sitting there with spreadsheets open, and then realize I’ve been clenching my jaw the whole time, which is weird because it’s not like the spreadsheet is physically doing anything to me.\n\nBy the evening I’ll have this headache and stiffness, like I spent the day hauling equipment around the lab or moving boxes, except I didn’t. I just… sat there tense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it, I guess. Some nights I’m fine, or at least I think I am, and other nights I look at the clock and start doing the math of how much sleep I’d get if I fell asleep right then, which is probably not the best thing to do.\n\nBut, sorry, that reminds me—my roommate has this lamp that clicks weirdly when it cools down, and for a while I thought it was something in the wall, like an electrical thing, so I went down this whole rabbit hole about whether outlets can make noises. So… sleep, yeah, I’m not totally sure how to answer. It hasn’t been something I’ve measured, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around thinking, like, “I’m depressed,” exactly. Some days I feel pretty low, but then I start wondering if that’s just because everything feels backed up, like when laundry sits too long and then somehow it becomes this whole project.\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess… I don’t know if it’s “down or depressed a lot.” I’ve noticed feeling kind of heavy sometimes, but I haven’t really sorted out whether that’s sadness or just being worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I… I’m not totally sure. I don’t really keep track of it that way.\n\nLike, I can tell you if a sample changed color or if an instrument reading drifted, because there’s a number or something to point to, but with sadness it’s more slippery. Some days I feel off, but I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. So… I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. My shoulders and neck are kind of… always up here, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be looking at spreadsheets and suddenly realize my jaw is clenched so hard it feels ridiculous.\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll think I’ve been sitting still all day, but by the evening I have this stiffness and headache like I was carrying equipment around the lab or hauling boxes or something. Which, I mean, sometimes I do carry stuff, but this is even on days where I mostly just sat there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yeah, tired, definitely. Like, I can be in bed a full night, and I still wake up feeling like I already pulled an all-nighter, which is annoying because then I’m trying to start the day already behind.\n\nAt work, even something simple like labeling samples can feel like it takes way more effort than it should. And then I get home and try to cook dinner for my family, and it’s like my body is heavy and my brain is just… drained. I’ll stand there with a pan out and forget what step I was on. Well, not forget exactly, just feel too wiped to keep moving smoothly.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say nearly every day. “Down” is kind of broad, you know? Like sometimes I’ll just be sitting there and everything feels… gray, I guess, but then I’ll get distracted by some work thing, like a batch number not matching or a weird email, and I’m more focused on that than on whether I’m depressed.\n\nHopeless feels like a big word. I don’t know. I haven’t really been counting days. The past two weeks have just sort of blurred together.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you often had little interest or pleasure in doing things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I still do things, but sometimes it feels more like I’m just moving through the list than actually enjoying them.\n\nLike, I’ll put on a show or start messing with something at home, and then I’m thinking about whether I left something unfinished at work, or if I should’ve checked an email again. And then I realize I didn’t really watch the episode. But I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest, or if I’m just distracted. \n\n“Pleasure” is a weird word, honestly. I guess I haven’t been paying close attention to that.\n\nDoctor: During the past two weeks, have you felt sad or low for most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “most of the day nearly every day.” That sounds really specific, and I don’t think I’ve been tracking it like that.\n\nSome days I feel kind of low, I guess, but then I’ll get pulled into something else and I’m not really thinking about whether I’m sad. Like even yesterday I started trying to figure out if the sink was draining slower than usual, and then I spent like twenty minutes messing with that instead of doing what I was supposed to be doing. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I still kind of do the same things I usually do, but I’m not sure I’m noticing whether I’m enjoying them or not.\n\nLike, I’ll start something and then my mind goes somewhere else, and then it’s like—wait, was I even into that? It’s more like I get stuck in the worry itself.\n\nDoctor: Before all of this recent worrying started, did you have trouble paying attention or staying organized going back to childhood?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I remember school being… school, you know? I’d procrastinate on stuff sometimes, but everyone did. I don’t know if it was a “since childhood” kind of thing.\n\nMy mom used to keep this big calendar on the fridge with everyone’s stuff on it, and I guess I depended on that more than I thought, but—sorry, I’m not sure that answers it. I don’t really remember having some obvious problem with attention or organization before all this.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel keyed up or on edge?\n\nPatient: Yeah, a lot. Especially at work I feel kind of… wound up, like I can’t just sit in my chair normally. I’ll be tapping my foot or picking up my phone to check messages even when there’s no reason, and then I’ll get up and walk back to the instrument room like I needed something, but I don’t even really need to be there.\n\nAnd normal lab noises will make me jump sometimes, which is embarrassing because it’s just the lab doing lab things, you know? Like a door closing or something clicking and I’m suddenly on alert.\n\nDoctor: If the situation at work were resolved, do you think most of this worrying would go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, but I don’t think so. Work is a big part of it, like with the new lab partnership and worrying they’re going to cut my position, or that my data are wrong and I’ve messed something up.\n\nBut then my mind just jumps to other stuff—like bills piling up, or my family thinking I’m falling behind, or whether I’ve already ruined my chances at grad school. Even when one thing gets answered, there’s another thing right behind it. So… no, I don’t think fixing work would make most of it go away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Mostly in my shoulders and neck—like they’re just sort of locked up all day. I’ll be sitting there with spreadsheets open, and then realize I’ve been clenching my jaw the whole time, which is weird because it’s not like the spreadsheet is physically doing anything to me.\n\nBy the evening I’ll have this headache and stiffness, like I spent the day hauling equipment around the lab or moving boxes, except I didn’t. I just… sat there tense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble sleeping?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know. I haven’t really been tracking it, I guess. Some nights I’m fine, or at least I think I am, and other nights I look at the clock and start doing the math of how much sleep I’d get if I fell asleep right then, which is probably not the best thing to do.\n\nBut, sorry, that reminds me—my roommate has this lamp that clicks weirdly when it cools down, and for a while I thought it was something in the wall, like an electrical thing, so I went down this whole rabbit hole about whether outlets can make noises. So… sleep, yeah, I’m not totally sure how to answer. It hasn’t been something I’ve measured, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around thinking, like, “I’m depressed,” exactly. Some days I feel pretty low, but then I start wondering if that’s just because everything feels backed up, like when laundry sits too long and then somehow it becomes this whole project.\n\nSorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess… I don’t know if it’s “down or depressed a lot.” I’ve noticed feeling kind of heavy sometimes, but I haven’t really sorted out whether that’s sadness or just being worn out.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad most days?\n\nPatient: Most days? I… I’m not totally sure. I don’t really keep track of it that way.\n\nLike, I can tell you if a sample changed color or if an instrument reading drifted, because there’s a number or something to point to, but with sadness it’s more slippery. Some days I feel off, but I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly. So… I don’t know.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically tense?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. My shoulders and neck are kind of… always up here, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be looking at spreadsheets and suddenly realize my jaw is clenched so hard it feels ridiculous.\n\nIt’s weird because I’ll think I’ve been sitting still all day, but by the evening I have this stiffness and headache like I was carrying equipment around the lab or hauling boxes or something. Which, I mean, sometimes I do carry stuff, but this is even on days where I mostly just sat there.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel tired or easily fatigued?\n\nPatient: Yeah, tired, definitely. Like, I can be in bed a full night, and I still wake up feeling like I already pulled an all-nighter, which is annoying because then I’m trying to start the day already behind.\n\nAt work, even something simple like labeling samples can feel like it takes way more effort than it should. And then I get home and try to cook dinner for my family, and it’s like my body is heavy and my brain is just… drained. I’ll stand there with a pan out and forget what step I was on. Well, not forget exactly, just feel too wiped to keep moving smoothly.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you felt down, depressed, or hopeless nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say nearly every day. “Down” is kind of broad, you know? Like sometimes I’ll just be sitting there and everything feels… gray, I guess, but then I’ll get distracted by some work thing, like a batch number not matching or a weird email, and I’m more focused on that than on whether I’m depressed.\n\nHopeless feels like a big word. I don’t know. I haven’t really been counting days. The past two weeks have just sort of blurred together.\n\nDoctor: Over the past two weeks, have you often had little interest or pleasure in doing things?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I still do things, but sometimes it feels more like I’m just moving through the list than actually enjoying them.\n\nLike, I’ll put on a show or start messing with something at home, and then I’m thinking about whether I left something unfinished at work, or if I should’ve checked an email again. And then I realize I didn’t really watch the episode. But I don’t know if that means I’ve lost interest, or if I’m just distracted. \n\n“Pleasure” is a weird word, honestly. I guess I haven’t been paying close attention to that.\n\nDoctor: During the past two weeks, have you felt sad or low for most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “most of the day nearly every day.” That sounds really specific, and I don’t think I’ve been tracking it like that.\n\nSome days I feel kind of low, I guess, but then I’ll get pulled into something else and I’m not really thinking about whether I’m sad. Like even yesterday I started trying to figure out if the sink was draining slower than usual, and then I spent like twenty minutes messing with that instead of doing what I was supposed to be doing. So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you lost interest in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I still kind of do the same things I usually do, but I’m not sure I’m noticing whether I’m enjoying them or not.\n\nLike, I’ll start something and then my mind goes somewhere else, and then it’s like—wait, was I even into that? But I don’t think I can clearly say I’ve “lost interest.” I just haven’t really been paying attention to it that way.\n\nDoctor: In the past two weeks, have you felt hopeless?\n\nPatient: Hopeless… I don’t know. I’m not sure I’d use that word. Like, I get stuck thinking things won’t go right, but hopeless sounds really big, like there’s no point to anything, and I don’t know if that’s what I mean.\n\nI mean, even the word makes me think of—sorry, this is probably off topic—but I had this one lab class where if the first measurement was off, the whole thing felt ruined, and I’d spend the rest of the time trying to fix it even though everyone else had moved on. So it’s kind of like that sometimes, where I feel like things are already messed up, but I don’t know if that’s hopeless. I guess I haven’t really noticed it in that exact way.\n\nDoctor: Is this worry causing problems with how you function day to day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s definitely messing things up.\n\nI missed an internal deadline at the lab because I just couldn’t get the writing organized or review the data in a normal way. I kept going back over things, trying to make sure it all made sense, and then somehow it was just… not done.\n\nAnd it’s not only work. Even regular stuff feels hard to keep up with, like preparing reports, or going to those art nights I usually show up for. Even cooking for my family has started feeling like this whole huge thing. Which is weird, because it’s not like any one piece of it is impossible, but together it feels like too much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
